In order to facilitate the rapid response\\r\\nof water utilities and to reduce water losses caused by undiscovered leakages, a timely detection\\r\\nmethod is required. Built on data-driven method and hydraulic modelling, this study proposes a\\r\\nmultistage approach to solve the leakage detection and localization of the battle problem in L-town.\\r\\nThe proposed approach is shown in fig.1, which consists of three stages:\\r\\ni)\\r\\nestimation of flow and pressure;\\r\\nii)\\r\\nidentification of abrupt and incipient leakages; and\\r\\niii)\\r\\nisolation of pipes with leakages.\\r\\n\\r\\nFigure 1. Flow chart of the multistage approach\\r\\n1. Estimation of flow and pressure\\r\\nAs described in the L-town problem statement, the water utility has set up pressure and flow\\r\\nsensors at a few predefined locations in the network. Time series data collected from these sensors\\r\\nare provided. The variations in the pressure/flow data are caused by daily, weekly and seasonal water\\r\\nusage patterns, leakages and other uncertain factors. To facilitate the identification of leak-induced\\r\\ndata from the time series, flow and pressure caused by the users, which are called flow and pressures\\r\\nunder normal condition, are estimated by data-driven time series methods.\\r\\nPAGE 2\\r\\n1.1 Flow\\r\\nAccording to the layout of the L-town water distribution network and the locations of the sensors,\\r\\nthe network is divided into two relatively independent areas. Area C has sensors for total inflow, and\\r\\nmost nodes in the area have water meters (AMRs) at the same time, which is regarded as one zone.\\r\\nArea A and B are treated as one zone, which are called area AB, because there is no separate sensor\\r\\nmeasuring the inflow to area B. We calculate the flow in area C and area AB separately.\\r\\nFor area C, the difference between the total inflow and total water consumption can reflect the\\r\\nleak information. The total inflow is calculated via the water level in the tank and the flow data of the\\r\\npump that delivers water to the Tank. It should be noted that the level data of the tank has not changed\\r\\nbefore and after some time point, and the pump at this time also stopped running. This seems to\\r\\nsuggest no water usage in the area, yet it contradicts with the data from the AMRs which show evident\\r\\nwater consumption in that period. This implies inaccuracy in the level data of the tower. To minimize\\r\\nits adverse impact on the detection of leakage events in area C, the total inflow and total demand in\\r\\narea C are calculated at intervals of 30 minutes.\\r\\nThe total inflow to the AB area, is recorded by three flow sensors . The actual water consumption\\r\\nof users in the AB area needs to be estimated. Considering the relatively complete monitoring of\\r\\njunction consumption behavior in area C, this can be used as a basis for the prediction of total demand\\r\\nin area AB. Specifically, the changing trend of users' water usage mode in area C during the year\\r\\nshould be similar to that in area AB. Therefore, the annual trend in the data in area C can be\\r\\ntransplanted to the prediction of water demand in area AB. The water consumption of area AB can\\r\\nbe predicted by combining with the simulation in one week of the hydraulic model. The main problem\\r\\nhere is to analyze the trend of demand mode in area C.\\r\\nWe assume that the demand in area C is composed of trend items (changes within the year),\\r\\nperiodic items (changes within the week), and random items (by stochastic factors), that is\\r\\n(t)\\r\\n(t)\\r\\n(t)\\r\\n(t)\\r\\nY\\r\\nS\\r\\nT\\r\\nR\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n(1)\\r\\nwhere Y(t) represents the total AMR data in the C area, S(t) represents the trend term, T(t) represents\\r\\nthe periodic term, and R(t) represents the random term in the data.\\r\\nTo extract the time series items, empirical mode decomposition (EMD) has been employed in\\r\\nthis study. EMD can identify the periodic component of time-series data and the trend items of data,\\r\\nit can decompose the original signal via subtracting the intrinsic modal function (IMF) successively,\\r\\nwhich is the mean of the upper and lower envelopes of the signal to be decomposed [2]. The trend\\r\\nitems in demand mode can be represented by one of the IMFs. The trend item in the AMR data is\\r\\nstripped through the multiplication model (Equation 1). Hence, the AMR data needs to be transformed.\\r\\nThe logarithmic transformation is used before EMD in this study. Then the trend item is extracted\\r\\nfrom AMR data of region C. This trend item represents the annual change in water consumption in\\r\\narea C (or area AB). In summary, the estimated value of water consumption in the AB area can be\\r\\ncalculated by the following method\\r\\n,\\r\\n( )\\r\\n( )\\r\\nAB\\r\\nAB predicted\\r\\ni\\r\\ni N\\r\\nDemand\\r\\nS t\\r\\nq t\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n(2)\\r\\nwhere qi(t) represents the demand at node i, which is obtained through the hydraulic model. NAB\\r\\nrepresent the node set of region AB.\\r\\nNote that areas with industrial users do not follow the same consumption pattern of the others,\\r\\nhence the AMR data does not include the records of the industrial junctions, which consume huge\\r\\namounts of water and have high uncertainty. These characteristics will affect the similarity of the two\\r\\nregional (area AB and area C) trend items.\\r\\n1.2 Pressure\\r\\nThe time series of pressure sensors are influenced by both normal water usages and water\\r\\nleakages. In order to select a suitable forecasting model for normal water consumption, the monitoring\\r\\ndata is comprehensively analyzed first [3]. The input data, which is a high dimensional time series\\r\\ndata, exhibits strong spatial and temporal correlation [4]. Vector autoregressive model (VAR) has\\r\\nproven to be efficient in many multi-dimensional time series forecasting tasks. VAR model\\r\\nregressively uses historical information and white noise to give the prediction of the current value. In\\r\\nPAGE 3\\r\\nthe meanwhile, the effects of exogenous series are considered as well. As such, we use VAR model\\r\\nto predict the normal pattern of the pressure series. In the training phase, we use a machine learning\\r\\nmodel trained only with the normal data. Then in the test phase, the well-trained model is applied on\\r\\nthe whole dataset and produces the normal pattern of the pressure series. After that, we use the\\r\\nresidual between the observed value and the true value in the detection of abnormal value. The\\r\\nresidual between the estimated series and the observed series indicates the abnormal behavior of the\\r\\npressure series.\\r\\n2. Identification\\r\\nFlow and pressure are estimated though appropriate prediction, the estimated values are\\r\\nconsidered as the aggregated user consumption under normal conditions. When a leakage occurs, the\\r\\nabnormal flow and pressure (observed from SCADA) differ from the estimated value, as shown in\\r\\nfig.2. For the flow of area C, data observed from the pump sensor and the tank level meter are used\\r\\nto calculate the total inflow of area C, and the sum of AMR demands gives the aggregate user\\r\\nconsumptions. The difference between total inflow and user consumptions is used to identify leakages\\r\\nas residuals in area C.\\r\\n\\r\\nFigure 2. Schematic diagram of leakage identification in Area AB. (a) Estimation of the flow; (b)\\r\\nResidual between estimated and observed flow; (c) Residuals between estimated and observed\\r\\npressures.\\r\\nIn the pipe networks provided, the leakages are divided into two types: abrupt leakage, incipient\\r\\nleakage. Corresponding to residual data, the pattern of residual series contains constant (no leak),\\r\\nabrupt increase (abrupt leakage), incipient increase (incipient leakage) and abrupt decrease (repaired\\r\\nleakage, the end time of leakage) . Different methods are proposed to deal with leakages of different\\r\\ntypes. Statistical Process Control (SPC) methods are proposed to solve this leakage detection mission.\\r\\nAs shown in fig. 2b, although the residuals series is calculated by the difference between\\r\\nobserved and estimated series, it is still a time series with irregular fluctuations. In order to reduce\\r\\nthe nonstationary of residual series, first difference and weekly difference are calculated to make the\\r\\ndata smooth, as shown in fig. 3. The abrupt increase and decrease are easily identified through\\r\\ndifference transformation, three-sigma SPC limit is used to identify the sudden rise and incline of the\\r\\nflow curve [5]. Therefore, the start time (abrupt increase), the end time of leakages (abrupt decrease),\\r\\nand the leak size (the abrupt value of increase or decrease) are obtained.\\r\\nPAGE 4\\r\\nFigure 3. Weekly difference of flow residuals in area AB.\\r\\nThere is one more thing to figure out, how does these start time match the end time. The equality\\r\\nof the variation of the flow at the start and end of the leakage is used to match the start time and the\\r\\nend time of a leakage. A complementing method to address this problem is to sort the pressure nodes\\r\\nresiduals, as shown in fig. 4. A sudden increase in pressure will lead to an increase in the residual\\r\\nbetween the current value and its moving average. The variation of the pressure at the neighboring\\r\\nnodes will be higher than more distant nodes. The birth and fix of a certain leakage will cause similar\\r\\npatterns for the neighbor nodes and the distant nodes. By observing the pattern at each start time and\\r\\nend time, a matching could be done.\\r\\n\\r\\nFigure 4. Variation of the pressure for the matching of the start time and end time.\\r\\nFor incipient leakages, a window-size based SPC slope method is employed to identify the\\r\\nincipient increase in flow residuals. If without leakages, the curve of residuals is likely to be smooth\\r\\nand constant. The pattern of flow residuals consists of many sub-patterns caused by different leakages.\\r\\nAfter the identification of abrupt leakages, the pattern caused by abrupt leakages could be removed\\r\\nfrom the curve of residuals. The rest of the curve only contains the sub-pattern caused by the incipient\\r\\nleakages. Their slopes could be used to distinguish different unfixed incipient leakages. Each incipient\\r\\nleakage has an identical and constant slope. In this way, the start time of each incipient leakage could\\r\\nbe identified once the slope of the curve changes. We use a slide window of length T to go through\\r\\nthe curve. As it moves along the time axis, it calculates the slope in the window. A threshold is set\\r\\nfor the identification of the incipient leakage. As shown in Table 1, all the unfixed incipient leakage\\r\\ncould be identified with the above mentioned method. However, the peak of each unfixed incipient\\r\\nleakages is hard to be determined because of the overlap of different incipient leakage. Therefore, an\\r\\napproximate peak time is given for each incipient leakage.\\r\\nTable 1. Identified slopes of the incipient leakages in area AB\\r\\nStart time\\r\\nSlope(m^3/(h*5min))\\r\\nPeak time\\r\\nLeak type\\r\\n2018/09/10 00:00\\r\\n0.000508\\r\\n2018/11/01 21:30\\r\\nIncipient leakage\\r\\n2019/03/28 14:15\\r\\n0.0014\\r\\n2019/04/07 14:15\\r\\nIncipient leakage\\r\\n2019/06/14 15:10\\r\\n0.00072\\r\\n2019/10/22 15:10\\r\\nIncipient leakage\\r\\n2019/10/25 22:20\\r\\n0.0015\\r\\n2019/12/09 22:20\\r\\nIncipient leakage\\r\\n2019/11/09 01:10\\r\\n0.0017\\r\\n2019/11/29 01:10\\r\\nIncipient leakage\\r\\n3. Localization\\r\\nDue to the fact that the boundaries of the three regions of the L-Town network are obvious, it is\\r\\neasy to build hydraulic models of area C as it has a large number of AMRs, so we split the L-Town\\r\\nPAGE 5\\r\\nnetwork into two parts, area C network and area AB network, to locate leakages separately. We first\\r\\ndelete the pump connecting area AB and area C, so that the original L-Town network is divided. For\\r\\narea AB network, we add a node at the position of the pump with a demand pattern based on pump\\r\\nflow SCADA data, and we replace the tank with a reservoir which is assigned a head pattern using\\r\\nthe observed tank level data in area C. Finally, we build two separated networks (areas AB and C)\\r\\nto locate leakages with the same hydraulic calculation results as the L-Town network.\\r\\nThe hydraulic model of L-town built on EPANET tool is employed to simulate nodal pressure.\\r\\nThe pressure sensors' data without leakages in 2018 is used to calibrate two networks by adjusting\\r\\nroughness of pipes to minimize the mean squared error (MSE) of observed and simulated pressure\\r\\nvalues.\\r\\nFor area AB, it is difficult to build an accurate hydraulic model as the network has no AMRs, so\\r\\nwe use the idea of control variates to localize each leakage in this area. Firstly, we find two weeks\\r\\nwhen the leakage had occurred and the leakage had not occurred based on the start and end time of\\r\\nthe leakage which is calculated before. Secondly, making a difference between the observed pressure\\r\\ndata at the corresponding time in the two weeks. We assume that the leakage to be localized is the\\r\\nonly leakage between the two weeks, so the difference could reflect the effect of this leakage on\\r\\npressure sensors in the network. Thirdly, two new area AB networks are created to indicate the two\\r\\nweeks mentioned above by adjusting demands with season trends and adding pump flow based on\\r\\nSCADA data, and some known unfixed leakages are added to the network as well. Then grid\\r\\nsearching is conducted for nodes and emitter coefficient. Nodes are searched one by one in area AB\\r\\nnetwork except for reservoir nodes. Emitter coefficients which are used to simulate leakages are\\r\\nsearched at appropriate intervals (based on the size of the leakage flow, i.e. bigger leakage flow has\\r\\nbigger search intervals). A difference between the simulated pressure data is calculated in the same\\r\\nway as before for the observed data. Finally, MSE of two differences, the observed values’ difference\\r\\nand the simulated values’ difference, is used as evaluation function for this problem, and we could\\r\\nfind high probability area of leakage with low MSE value.\\r\\nMSE =\\r\\n∑\\r\\n(∑\\r\\n(∆𝑃𝑜𝑖,𝑗−∆𝑃𝑠𝑖,𝑗)2\\r\\n𝑇\\r\\n𝑗=1\\r\\n)\\r\\n𝑁𝑠\\r\\n𝑖=1\\r\\n𝑁𝑠∙𝑇\\r\\n(3)\\r\\nWhere 𝑁𝑠 is the total number of pressure sensors in area AB, 𝑇 is the total number of\\r\\nsimulation time steps, ∆𝑃𝑜𝑖,𝑗 is the difference of two weeks’ observed value at pressure sensor 𝑖 at\\r\\ntime 𝑗 and ∆𝑃𝑠𝑖,𝑗 is the difference of two weeks’ simulated value at pressure sensor 𝑖 at time 𝑗.\\r\\nIn area C, almost every node has an AMR, which facilitates accurate building of an hydraulic\\r\\nmodel in EPANET. We select one week when the bust had been occurred and compare the observed\\r\\nand the simulated values to determine the location of the leakage. Secondly, we assign the AMR\\r\\ndemand data to nodes and calculate the reservoir head pattern according to the SCADA data of tank\\r\\nlevel. Thirdly, search for nodes and emitter coefficient and find the difference between the observed\\r\\npressure value and the simulated value. Finally, we use the same way to calculate MSE of the\\r\\ndifference and find high probable area of leakage.\\r\\nFig.5 shows the result of the leakage occurred at 20th April 2019, 12:00. The shade in grey in\\r\\nthe figure represents the size of the MSE. The node with darker color has smaller MSE. Among\\r\\nthem, red, orange, yellow, green and blue indicate the five nodes with the smallest MSE, that is, the\\r\\narea with the highest probability of leakage.\\r\\nPAGE 6\\r\\nFigure 5. Schematic diagram of leakage isolation.\\r\\n4. Conclusion\\r\\nThis study presents a method for leakage detection and isolation for the battle of L-town DMA.\\r\\nFirstly, data-driven estimations of flow and pressure are proposed to extract leak-induced data from\\r\\nsensor data. Empirical mode decomposition is performed to obtain the time series trend item and\\r\\nestimate flow under normal conditions. Vector auto regressive is employed to extract the spatial\\r\\ncorrelation of pressure sensors and estimate the time series data of pressure. Secondly, first difference\\r\\nand weekly difference are calculated to reduce the nonstationary of residual series. For abrupt\\r\\nleakages, the duration and size of leakages are identified by 3-sigma SPC method, and the start time\\r\\nand the end time are matched by analysing the leak size and pressure pattern. For the detection of\\r\\nincipient leakages, which usually yield gentle slope and small quantity of water loss, the window-size\\r\\nslope variation method is used in this study. Thirdly, the emitter coefficient of node in EPANET is\\r\\nset to simulate pipe leakage. The idea of control variates is used to find the two weeks when leakage\\r\\nhad occurred and not occurred respectively. The difference between the pressure data of two weeks\\r\\nis calculated to represent the impact of the leakage on the pressure of the network. By the above\\r\\nmethod, 16 leakages are detected and localized in the battle DMA networks in 2019.\\r\\nPAGE 7\\r\\nA multistage approach to detect and isolate multiple leakages in\\r\\ndistrict metering areas in water distribution systems\\r\\n\\r\\nXiaoting Wang1*, Junyu Li2, Xipeng Yu3, Ziqing Ma4, Yujun Huang5\\r\\n1,2,3,4,5 Smart Water Research Center, School of Environment, Tsinghua University, Beijing, China\\r\\n1 wang-xt15@mails.tsinghua.edu.cn;\\r\\n\\r\\nSUMMARY\\r\\nHydraulic accidents or abnormal situations, also known as leakages, cause not only water losses,\\r\\nbut also service interruptions and other negative effects. In order to facilitate the rapid response of\\r\\nwater utilities and reduce water losses caused by undiscovered leakages, a timely detection and\\r\\nisolation method is required. To solve the battle problem in L-town, this study investigates the\\r\\npotential of the combination use of data-driven method and hydraulic modelling and proposes a\\r\\nmultistage approach, which comprises three stages: estimation, identification and localization. Firstly,\\r\\nempirical mode decomposition and vector auto regressive are performed to identify the trend in flow\\r\\ntime series and the spatial correlation of pressure values at different sensors. The two data-driven\\r\\nestimations of flow and pressure are used to extract leak-induced values from the monitoring data.\\r\\nSecondly, first difference and weekly difference are calculated to reduce the nonstationary of residual\\r\\nseries. The duration and size of leakages are identified by analysing residuals though three-sigma\\r\\nSPC method. Meanwhile, the window-size slope variation method is proposed to detect the start time\\r\\nand leak size of incipient leakages. Thirdly, emitters are used to represent and simulate pipe leakages\\r\\nin EPANET2. Localization algorithm, which uses the idea of control variates to find two weeks when\\r\\nthe leakage had occurred and not occurred, is employed to represent the impact of the leakage on the\\r\\npressure of the network by calculating the difference between the pressure data of two weeks. By\\r\\nidentifying the gaps between the differences of the observed values and…5708 tokens truncated… self-attention mechanism, the model can learn and transfer the pressure characteristics from sensor nodes to all nodes, rather than being limited to the sensor nodes’ neighborhood. Therefore, spatial generalizing process can be summarized as two significant mechanisms: neighbor topology feature transferring based on graph convolution, and global feature generalization based on the self-attention mechanism. Based on these two key mechanisms, the AGN model can generalize the learned graph structure-related features to all nodes in WDS, achieving state forecasting.\\n\\nIn the GGNN, ensuring sensor nodes can “perceive” the farthest non-sensor nodes theoretically requires up to 11 steps of recurrent iterations. However, this approach presents significant practical challenges, particularly due to the substantial increase in training time. For example, training with 11 iterations of recurrent layers on an A10 tensor core GPU in the small-scale network studied here takes about two days. This duration is unacceptable for practical applications in large-scale WDSs, where non-sensor nodes are further away from their nearest sensor node. In contrast, using 3 recurrent layers reduces the training time to about 3 h, demonstrating significant efficiency advantages.\\n\\nFurther analysis reveals that increasing the number of recurrent layers from 1 to 2 significantly reduces the MAPE. However, increasing beyond 3 layers leads to a rise in MAPE. This suggests that too many recurrent layers may introduce excessive non-sensor node features, causing data redundancy or pollution, and weakening the model’s prediction performance. Additionally, regardless of the number of recurrent layers, the cumulative maximum MAE remains stable at 96.03 %, revealing an optimization bottleneck in specific regions, such as low-elevation region in this study. These regions, due to insufficient sensor placement, cannot improve training results by increasing the number of recurrent layers.\\n\\nIn summary, incorporating the self-attention mechanism in the recurrent layers significantly enhances the model’s prediction accuracy. ## 5. Conclusions\\n\\nThis study proposes a novel multi-step demand forecasting solution for cities lacking hydraulic model support. By integrating forecasting and interpretability into a single, cohesive framework, it provides accurate and transparent predictions. Compared to other forecasting methods not reliant on hydraulic models, this study demonstrates the superiority of the proposed AGN method in both spatial generalization performance and its long-term accuracy in temporal forecasting performance. The high accuracy achieved further validates the feasibility of predicting water demand states from pressure measurements, offering a novel approach to demand forecasting. To the best of our knowledge, this work represents a pioneering effort to address interpretability within complex WDS forecasting models. By interpreting the model, this study gained deeper insights of the internal working mechanisms of complex network prediction models. Additionally, the interpretability framework aids model refinement, improving alignment with actual WDS behavior and enhancing overall predictive and interpretative performance.\\n\\nIn a sustainable and cost-effective WDS, this research offers a valuable tool for efficient demand modeling for numerous cities lacking hydraulic models. Our research findings have practical implications beyond the benchmark we considered: cities seeking to optimize water distribution and enhance the management level of WDSs will benefit from the insights provided by our framework. The findings hold practical significance extending beyond the specific case studies presented: Cities aiming to optimize water distribution and improve management practices will benefit from the insights offered by our framework. Water utilities can leverage the interpretable predictions to enhance confidence in their scheduling decisions.\\n\\nHowever, this study still has certain limitations and gaps in practical applications: (1) potential information leakage during the model tuning process when determining optimal parameters based on model performance; (2) uncertainties related to sensor acquisition errors and infrastructure conditions; and (3) the inability to satisfy all three conservation equations at the same time.\\n\\nOur open-source model lays a valuable foundation for developing machine learning-based models as an alternative to hydraulic models, which future research can further optimize. Future research should focus on enhancing the architecture of GNN and applying more interpretability methods to improve both performance and clarity. Model Performance: First, the model should be tested on more real-world WDSs to verify its robustness and explore the relationship between network characteristics and optimal parameter settings. Additionally, more water network features should be extracted, and various types of sensor data should be integrated to achieve multi-source information integration, enhancing the model’s alignment with practical problems. Furthermore, incorporating physical laws can constrain the predictions, improving both the accuracy and credibility of the model. Model Interpretability: The transparency of the model directly affects its deplorability in the water distribution field, which is characterized by robustness and safety. This study primarily provides model-level explanations. However, for complex model architectures, global explanations may be difficult to understand in depth. To fully comprehend the model’s outputs, future research should develop instance-level explanations that align better with human cognitive habits and are easier for non-technical users to understand. Examples of such methods include perturbation-based, gradient/feature-based, and decomposition-based approaches.\\n\\n## CRediT authorship contribution statement\\n\\nZekun Zou: Writing – review & editing, Writing – original draft, Software, Methodology, Investigation, Formal analysis, Conceptualization. Liu\\n\\nPost-hoc interpretability and explanations\\n\\nExplainable Artificial Intelligence: An Introduction to Interpretable Machine Learning, Springer International Publishing, Cham (2021), pp. 167-216, 10.1007/978-3-030-83356-5_5\\n## Cited by (4)\\n\\n  * ### Spatio-temporal network risk evolution of PM2.5 transmission pathways considering volatility\\n\\n2025, Results in Engineering\\n\\nShow abstract\\n\\nHaze pollution, driven by PM_{2.5}, poses significant risks to public health and environmental sustainability, particularly in urban-industrial regions like the Fenwei Plain, China. This study introduces the Lasso-Cox Dynamic Attention Network (LCDAN), a novel multimodal framework for modeling PM_{2.5} transmission risks. By integrating Generalized Autoregressive Conditional Heteroskedasticity (GARCH (1,1)) for volatility analysis, graph neural networks (GNNs) with attention mechanisms for spatial path representation, extended Long Short-Term Memory (xLSTM) for temporal dynamics, and SHAP for interpretability, LCDAN captures the complex spatio-temporal evolution of PM_{2.5} propagation networks. Using a comprehensive dataset (2019–2023) encompassing air pollutants, meteorological, socio-economic, and geographic data, the model outperforms eight benchmarks, achieving a 5.5% average F1 score improvement and up to 38% for low-risk scenarios. A case study in Xi’an (November–December 2023) demonstrates LCDAN’s ability to identify critical transmission pathways and risk nodes, with SHAP analysis highlighting industrial emissions and economic density as key drivers. The model’s efficiency (42-minute training, <15 ms online inference) and interpretability make it a robust tool for air quality management, offering actionable insights for policymakers to mitigate haze pollution effectively.\\n\\n  * ### Diagnosing the Roles of Meteorological Forcing, Storage Memory, and Network Connectivity in Cold-Region Streamflow Prediction\\n\\n2026, Water Resources Management\\n\\n  * ### Enhanced Coordinated Sensor Network for Real-Time Lake Kivu Water Quality Monitoring\\n\\n2026, International Journal of Safety and Security Engineering\\n\\n  * ### A physics-informed AI methodology for Prognostics and Health Management of complex systems under low data constraints\\n\\n2026, Journal of Intelligent Manufacturing\\n\\n© 2025 The Authors. To tackle this problem, this approach\\r\\nincludes some data normalization to extract the most meaningful features on daily bases, transforming\\r\\nthe initial 5 minutes sample into the following attributes:\\r\\n\\r\\n• Minimum peak: Consists of the minimum daily registered value.\\r\\n• Maximum peak: Gathering the highest daily value\\r\\n• Mean daily: The average value without including the nights. The suggested solution does not implement any process for outlier’s removal or\\r\\nresampling values, due to the nature of the problem it has been assumed that all values are completely\\r\\nreliable. Consists of dividing the data set into folds\\r\\nand using only one of them as a test set and the rest of them as train set. This process is\\r\\nrepeated iteratively until all folds are treated as test set separately. To generate unbiased\\r\\nresults, the purposed solution constructs folds with the same number of sequential days.\\r\\n\\r\\n• Leakage detection: This second step takes as inputs the previously gathered predictions to\\r\\nestablish comparisons within the real mean night pressure and the current predictions. The established threshold for separating leakages\\r\\nand normal pressure values is -0.015. Ultimately, an adjacent pipe is manually selected based\\r\\non the obtained leakage for the neighbor pressure sensor at the same specific time.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe class attribute of the machine learning algorithm is the mean pressure during the night. This provides a rich data source for the AGN model and practically verifies the applicability and feasibility of water demand prediction from pressure data. ...   * Journal of Hydrology: Regional Studies, Volume 61, 2025, Article 102665\n\n## Results in Engineering\n\nVolume 27, September 2025, 106911\n\n# \n\nResearch paper\n\nExplainable graph neural network for real-time demand states forecasting in water distribution system\n\nhttps://doi.org/10.1016/j.rineng.2025.106911Get rights and content\n\nUnder a Creative Commons license\n\nOpen access\n\n## Highlights\n\n  * •\n\nAttention-enhanced GNN for real-time demand forecasting based on pressure data.\n\n  * •\n\nModel is independent from hydraulic model, offering broader applicability.\n\n  * •\n\nModel exhibits high spatiotemporal accuracy and sustained long-term effectiveness.\n\n  * •\n\nModel explanation reveals its operational mechanisms, facilitating field deployment.\n\n## Abstract\n\nEfficient real-time nodal demand forecasting in water distribution systems is essential for a reliable and resilient water supply. Given the efficiency of the data-driven approach and the intricate node-line structure of water networks, graph-based machine learning methods offer a promising approach. However, the scarcity of real-time flow acquisition devices limits the ability to directly forecast timely demand. Pressure sensors provide a more practical alternative. Thus, this study proposes a novel attention-augmented gated graph neural network (AGN) for real-time demand forecasting from a pressure perspective. By integrating attention mechanisms, the AGN overcomes limitations of convolution-based graph neural networks, such as capturing long-range dependencies and dynamic node interactions, leading to enhanced performance on real-world modeling problems. This model is formulated for real and sensitive contexts where, in addition to being accurate, the predictions must also be understandable by human operators. Thus, model-level explanations are then introduced in our explainable framework. The proposed AGN outperforms benchmark methods in both spatiotemporal accuracy and long-term forecasting effectiveness, while simultaneously delivering interpretable insights into the demand forecasting process. The novel approach based on the fusion of forecasting and explainability in a single framework enables the creation of powerful and reliable systems suitable for real-world issues and challenges.\n\n## 1. Introduction\n\nAs urban populations and city sizes continue to grow, urban water distribution systems (WDSs), as a fundamental component for ensuring public welfare, are facing increasing complexity and management challenges [10,25]. To maintain the stability of WDSs, accurate forecasting and proactive preparation for future demands are essential [11,35]. The predictive lead time establishes critical temporal buffers for municipal water operators, strengthening their operational resilience. However, short-term demand forecasting in real-life WDSs proves challenging due to the need for predictions at all nodes based on limited sensor layouts and complex network topology. The stochastic variability of water usage, driven by human activities, complicates the forecasting process [26]. Given the hurdles in distribution reliability and the capricious nature of water consumption, accurate real-time demand forecasting is crucial for ensuring a reliable and resilient WDS [12].\nConsequently, the primary objective of this study focuses on developing an innovative state estimation framework that operates independently of conventional hydraulic modeling paradigms.\n\nSeveral studies [18,33,37] have enabled direct modeling of WDSs, obviating the need for hydraulic models. Based on the assumption of smooth variations in nodal head (the sum of nodal pressure and elevation of a node), Zhou et al. [37] developed a graph-based head reconstruction (GHR) method. This method decomposes the hydraulic head into components with different variation speeds, reconstructing the head using the slowly varying components obtained from a limited number of monitoring nodes. However, this method heavily relies on having a sufficient number of well-defined sensor nodes and a regular underlying graph structure. Xing and Sela [33] were the pioneers in employing a graph neural network (GNN) to achieve state estimation within WDSs. They developed an encoder-decoder GNN architecture, enabling real-time synchronous estimation of pipeline flow and nodal pressure states. These methods are categorized into model-level and instance-level explanations. The former elucidates the behavior and decision-making process of the entire model, while the latter provides input-specific explanations for each graph instance. Model-based explanations offer a macroscopic understanding of the overall model behavior by identifying general patterns and rules under different input scenarios. Within this context, the concept of explainability is tied to specific model fragments. These explanations delve into the inference-making process of the model, providing a comprehensive account of which features significantly impact the inference results [14,28]. These techniques analyze the inference process to determine which features significantly influence outputs, enabling retrospective examination of parameter contributions. Model-level explanations can be further divided into three parts: rule extraction, hidden semantics analysis, and attribution methods [20].\n\nThis study innovative proposes an explainable attention-augmented gated graph neural network (AGN) framework for water demand states forecasting from a pressure perspective. Derived from GRU-based GNN (i.e. GGNN), this model not only exhibits excellent time series prediction performance but also captures the network’s structure. The innovative attention mechanisms are then incorporated to address the limitation of GGNN in capturing solely local dependencies and enhance the model's interpretability through its transparent decision-making process. Overall, the paper encompasses the following novelties:\n\n·Demand forecasting model designed for water network characteristics: A core innovation of this study is the development of the AGN model, specifically designed to capture the unique spatio-temporal characteristics of water distribution networks. The proposed AGN model forecasts future demands at all nodes, without relying on hydraulic models. The AGN model shows significant accuracy in both spatial and temporal dimensions compared to existing direct modeling methods. The model maintains high predictive robustness across various forecasting horizons. Our high accuracy results validate the feasibility and efficiency of demand prediction from a pressure perspective.\n\nModel explanation associated with water network characteristics: Merely achieving sufficient prediction accuracy is not enough to guarantee the model’s deployment in real-world WDSs. To bridge this gap, the second innovation addresses the crucial need for improved model transparency. Specifically, the key lies in integrating model explanations with practical engineering issues, enabling network managers to understand and trust the model, thereby making reliable decisions. To the best knowledge, we are the first to address interpretability issues in WDS. By providing these explanations, this study provided a deeper understanding of the roles of different model components in the prediction task. This process also aids in improving the model by offering feedback to further enhance its predictive performance.\n\n## 2. Methodology\n\nThis study proposes an Attentive Graph Network (AGN) to forecast water demand in distribution systems using pressure data without hydraulic modeling. The architecture leverages the inherent graph structure of water networks, historical pressure measurements, and nodal features to predict future nodal demands. Specifically designed to capture unique pressure characteristics in water distribution systems (WDS), the model includes built-in interpretability mechanisms t"
  }
]
```

Iterating from [0] to\\r\\n[k] leads to\\r\\n\\r\\ne[k]\\r\\n\\r\\n≤¯σk−1\\r\\n\\r\\ne[0]\\r\\n\\r\\n+ (¯σ + . . . + ¯σk) ∥h∥=\\r\\n¯σk−1\\r\\n\\r\\ne[0]\\r\\n\\r\\n+ (¯σ −¯σk+1)/(1 −¯σ) ∥h∥. Applying Lemma 2\\r\\nfor\\r\\nmatrices\\r\\nM [k]\\r\\ns\\r\\nand\\r\\nF ,\\r\\nboth\\r\\nwith\\r\\nsubunitary\\r\\nreal\\r\\neigenvalues, we obtain that the product matrices M [k]\\r\\ns F and\\r\\nM [k]\\r\\ns\\r\\n(In −F ) also have this property. This allows to state\\r\\nthat 0 < ¯σ < 1, which introduced in the last inequality shows\\r\\nthat the estimation error is bounded in the initial error and the\\r\\nterm h and that, asymptotically (when k →∞), it depends\\r\\nonly on the second term, thus concluding the proof.\\r\\nRemark 2. The previous theoretical framework allows us\\r\\nto confirm the stability of the required Kalman Filter im-\\r\\nplementation, composed by a linear state prediction function\\r\\n(through a diffusion matrix as the state prediction matrix)\\r\\nand the availability of only head measurements. In the fol-\\r\\nlowing sections, the state estimation methodology is extended\\r\\nto include demand measurements, which are related to the\\r\\nsystem states (hydraulic heads) through a non-linear equation.\\r\\nTherefore, the linear theory in Section III-A help us to motivate\\r\\nthe promising performance of Kalman-based algorithms. The\\r\\nnecessary extension to address the problem of inclusion of non-\\r\\nlinear measurements is handled with the use of the Unscented\\r\\nKalman Filter. The analysis of the stability of this algorithm is\\r\\nbeyond of the scope of this article, although several results in\\r\\nthe state of the art analyze and reduce associated numerical\\r\\nproblems leading to instability, such as the development of\\r\\nupgraded algorithms like the square-root UKF [22], and the\\r\\ndevelopment of the UKF-related theory [24].\\r\\n♦\\r\\nB. Including demand measurements\\r\\nThe non-linear estimation capabilities of the UKF, explained\\r\\nin Section II-B2b, enable the integration of demand measure-\\r\\nments in the data assimilation function, as demonstrated with\\r\\nUKF-(AW)GSI in [16].\\r\\nIn this method, the prediction step employs a linear function,\\r\\nsimplifying the prediction process due to its equivalency with\\r\\nthe prediction operation in the KF for a linear prediction\\r\\nfunction f. This equivalence arises from the SUT’s ability to\\r\\nperfectly capture linearity without requiring further approxi-\\r\\nmations [25]:\\r\\nˆh[k]\\r\\n−= F ˆh[k−1],\\r\\nˆ\\r\\nP [k]\\r\\nh,−= F ˆ\\r\\nP [k−1]\\r\\nh\\r\\nF ⊤+ Qh\\r\\n(9)\\r\\nwhere F and Qh are respectively the state transition and\\r\\nprocess noise covariance matrices for the prediction in (3).\\r\\nNote that u[k−1] is removed because there is no input in\\r\\nthe process. In our case, the weighting parameter ϵ in the\\r\\ndefinition of F is defined as ϵ = nc\\r\\nn , where nc is the number of\\r\\nAMRs. This decision is justified by the importance of demand\\r\\ninformation within the estimation process. If demand data is\\r\\nabundant, the relative weight of the data assimilation process\\r\\nshould be higher, and thus we should maintain the state from\\r\\nthe previous iteration. Nonetheless, if demand data is scarce,\\r\\nthe prediction function can make use of the diffusion matrix\\r\\nto extend the corrected state estimates to unobserved regions.\\r\\nBased on UKF, a non-linear expression appears in the\\r\\nmeasurement propagation step:\\r\\nH[k]\\r\\n−=\\r\\nh\\r\\nˆh[k]\\r\\n−\\r\\nˆh[k]\\r\\n−± η\\r\\nq\\r\\nˆ\\r\\nP [k]\\r\\nh,−\\r\\ni\\r\\n(10a)\\r\\nY[k]\\r\\n−= g(H[k]\\r\\n−) =\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\nSH[k]\\r\\n−\\r\\n−\\r\\n\\u0010\\r\\nˆ\\r\\nB[k]\\r\\nc\\r\\n\\u0011⊤\\u0010\\r\\nT −1 ˆ\\r\\nB[k]H[k]\\r\\n−\\r\\n\\u0011\\r\\n1\\r\\n1.852\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n(10b)\\r\\nˆy[k]\\r\\n−=\\r\\n2n\\r\\nX\\r\\ni=0\\r\\nw(m)\\r\\ni\\r\\nY[k]\\r\\ni,−\\r\\n(10c)\\r\\nˆ\\r\\nP [k]\\r\\nyy =\\r\\n2n\\r\\nX\\r\\ni=0\\r\\nw(c)\\r\\ni\\r\\n\\u0010\\r\\nY[k]\\r\\ni,−−ˆy[k]\\r\\n−\\r\\n\\u0011 \\u0010\\r\\nY[k]\\r\\ni,−−ˆy[k]\\r\\n−\\r\\n\\u0011⊤\\r\\n+ Rh\\r\\n(10d)\\r\\nˆ\\r\\nP [k]\\r\\nxy =\\r\\n2n\\r\\nX\\r\\ni=0\\r\\nw(c)\\r\\ni\\r\\n\\u0010\\r\\nH[k]\\r\\ni,−−ˆh[k]\\r\\n−\\r\\n\\u0011 \\u0010\\r\\nY[k]\\r\\ni,−−ˆy[k]\\r\\n−\\r\\n\\u0011⊤\\r\\n(10e)\\r\\nwhere H[k]\\r\\n−is the set of head prior ”sigma” points and ˆ\\r\\nB[k] is\\r\\nan approximated incidence matrix, computed considering the\\r\\ncurrent hydraulic state of the network as:\\r\\nˆb[k]\\r\\noj =\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n\\r\\n−1,\\r\\nˆh[k]\\r\\ni\\r\\n≥ˆh[k]\\r\\nj\\r\\n(eo = (vi, vj) ∈E)\\r\\n1,\\r\\nˆh[k]\\r\\ni\\r\\n< ˆh[k]\\r\\nj\\r\\n(eo = (vj, vi) ∈E)\\r\\n0,\\r\\n(vi, vj) /∈E and (vj, vi) /∈E\\r\\n(11)\\r\\nMoreover, T ∈Rm×m is the resistance coefficient diagonal\\r\\nmatrix, whose diagonal is formed by τ, defined in Section\\r\\nII-A1. The specific non-linear relation in (10b) is derived from\\r\\nthe non-linear flow-head relation, defined by expressions such\\r\\nas the Hazen-Williams equation, posed as:\\r\\nq[k] =\\r\\n\\u0010\\r\\nT −1B[k]h[k]\\u0011\\r\\n1\\r\\n1.852 ,\\r\\n(12)\\r\\nand the linear demand-flow relation, defined as a mass con-\\r\\nservation equation at the network nodes, namely c[k]\\r\\n=\\r\\n−\\r\\n\\u0010\\r\\nˆ\\r\\nB[k]\\r\\nc\\r\\n\\u0011⊤\\r\\nq[k], where c[k] is the nodal demand vector and\\r\\nˆ\\r\\nB[k]\\r\\nc\\r\\nis a submatrix of ˆ\\r\\nB[k] where only the columns corre-\\r\\nsponding to the nodes with ARMs are selected. Improving flow estimation\\r\\nThe presented UKF-based scheme for the integration of\\r\\ndemand measurements showed its potential in [16]. The pro-\\r\\nposed algorithm only considers the estimation of hydraulic\\r\\nheads, which play the role of the network state representative.\\r\\nNevertheless, the proper estimation of other hydraulic vari-\\r\\nables such as flow could be of great importance for water\\r\\nutilities in network control operations. Therefore, we propose\\r\\nan extension to the methodology explained in Section III-B\\r\\nto integrate both head and flow estimation. Note that the\\r\\nestimatio…3229 tokens truncated…mprovement in various field. Inspired by the hu-\\r\\nman attention mechanism, the attention-based RNN model\\r\\nwas first proposed in a machine translation task [Bahdanau\\r\\net al., 2014].\\r\\nEmploying the encoder-decoder structure\\r\\nand the attention mechanism applied to the encoding se-\\r\\nquence, the model became, at that time, the start-of-the-\\r\\nart work in English-to-French translation. Later, the well-\\r\\nknown Transformer [Vaswani et al., 2017] dispensed with\\r\\nthe recurrent module and solely employed several Multi-\\r\\nHead attention modules and feed-forward modules. Proven\\r\\nefficient in sequence to sequence tasks like natural lan-\\r\\nguage process [Bahdanau et al., 2014; Vaswani et al., 2017;\\r\\nYang et al., 2016] and computer vision tasks [Xu et al., 2015;\\r\\nHuang et al., 2019], the attention-based models were also\\r\\nwidely used in time series problems. Dual-stage attention-\\r\\nbased RNN (DS-RNN) [Qin et al., 2017] employed two atten-\\r\\ntion modules for spatial-temporal correlation capturing and\\r\\noutperformed the other traditional nonlinear autoregressive\\r\\nexogenous models. GeoMAN [Liang et al., 2018] used three-\\r\\nstage attention respectively applied to local features, global\\r\\nfeatures and temporal sequence for geo-sensory series pre-\\r\\ndiction. To enhance the correlation of the target series and\\r\\nexogenous series, a dual-stage two-phase attention model,\\r\\nnamely DSTP-RNN [Liu et al., 2019], was proposed. The\\r\\nfirst-phase spatial attention was applied to the original series.\\r\\nThe second-phase spatial attention was applied to the con-\\r\\ncatenation of the former output and the target series. The\\r\\nsecond stage (temporal attention) was the same as the afore-\\r\\nmentioned works.\\r\\nHowever, among the current attention-based RNN models,\\r\\none essential bottleneck could be the information loss during\\r\\nthe training of attention weights. Specifically, the spatial at-\\r\\ntention mechanism only considers inputs along the temporal\\r\\naxis and ignores exogenous inputs. Hence, we proposed a\\r\\nhybrid spatial attention mechanism in the encoder stage. It\\r\\ncould simultaneously capture inputs along temporal and spa-\\r\\ntial axes to training the attention weights. To the best of our\\r\\nknowledge, no prior work has used a similar method in the\\r\\nspatial attention stage.\\r\\n3\\r\\nNotation & Problem statement\\r\\nGiven n ∈N+ series over a window length of T ∈N+ as\\r\\ninputs, we use xk\\r\\nt ∈R to represent the reading of k-th sensor\\r\\nat time t. We use Xt = (x1\\r\\nt, x2\\r\\nt, .., xn\\r\\nt )T ∈Rn×T to represent\\r\\nthe encoder input series of all geo-sensors at time t. In addi-\\r\\ntion, the output of the encoder layer is represented by Zt =\\r\\n(z1\\r\\nt , z2\\r\\nt , .., zm\\r\\nt )T ∈Rm×T , where m is the number of hidden\\r\\nstates. The reading of the target sensor at time tis represented\\r\\nby yt. Given τ as the window length of predicted series, the\\r\\ntarget series for prediction which ranges from T to T + τ can\\r\\nbe represented by Y = (yT +1, yT +2, ..., yT +τ)T ∈Rτ. The\\r\\nhistorical part of target series y1\\r\\nt , y2\\r\\nt , .., yn\\r\\nt is included in in-\\r\\nput series. We use ˆY = (ˆyT +1, ˆyT +2, ..., ˆyT +τ)T ∈Rτ to\\r\\nrepresent the predicted series.\\r\\nA mapping from historical multivariate series to the future\\r\\ntarget series is expected to be learned. Note F(·) as the map-\\r\\nping function of the model, the problem is defined as follows:\\r\\nˆyT +1, ˆyT +2, ..., ˆyT +τ = F(x1\\r\\nt, x2\\r\\nt, .., xn\\r\\nt )\\r\\n(1)\\r\\n4\\r\\nMethods\\r\\nThe overall framework of proposed hybrid DS-RNN is pre-\\r\\nsented in Figure 2. The encoder-decoder structure [Sutskever\\r\\nPAGE 3\\r\\nFigure 2: The framework of the proposed hybrid dual-stage at-\\r\\ntention networks (hDA-RNN). In the first stage, the spatial at-\\r\\ntention weights ak\\r\\nt is trained with the inputs along the tempo-\\r\\nral axis (xk\\r\\n1, xk\\r\\n2, .., xk\\r\\nT )T ∈Rn and those along the spatial axis\\r\\n(x1\\r\\nt, x2\\r\\nt, .., xn\\r\\nT )T ∈Rn. In addition, the hidden states and cell states\\r\\nof the previous LSTM unit he,t−1, se,t−1 are employed to train the\\r\\nspatial attention weights. The output of the encoder is represented as\\r\\nZt = (z1\\r\\nt , z2\\r\\nt , .., zm\\r\\nt )T ∈Rm×T . In the second stage, the temporal\\r\\nattention computes the attention weights with mid-layer states across\\r\\nall the time steps and the hidden states and the cell states of the de-\\r\\ncoder hd,t′−1, sd,t′−1. The output of the decoder is represented as\\r\\n(ˆyT +1, ˆyT +2, ..., ˆyT +τ)T ∈Rτ\\r\\net al., 2014], proven efficient in sequence to sequence mod-\\r\\nelling, is employed in our framework.\\r\\nThe LSTM unit\\r\\n[Hochreiter and Schmidhuber, 1997] is used as the basic unit\\r\\nin both encoder and decoder. A dual-stage attention mech-\\r\\nanism [Qin et al., 2017] i.e., a spatial attention layer in en-\\r\\ncoder and a temporal attention layer in decoder, is employed.\\r\\nSpecifically, a hybrid spatial attention mechanism is proposed\\r\\nin the first stage. In the second stage, the temporal attention\\r\\nmechanism is employed.\\r\\n4.1\\r\\nSpatial attention in encoder\\r\\nThe spatial attention mechanism is performed in the first\\r\\nstage.\\r\\nFollowing previous work, a dual-stage attention-\\r\\nbased RNN (DA-RNN)[Qin et al., 2017] serves as an im-\\r\\nportant baseline in our experiment. Firstly, we present the\\r\\nattention mechanism employed in DA-RNN. Given Xk =\\r\\n(xk\\r\\n1, xk\\r\\n2, .., xk\\r\\n3)T ∈RT as k-th input series and he,t−1, se,t−1\\r\\nas the hidden states and the cell states of the encoder LSTM\\r\\ncell at step t-1. The spatial attention mechanism can be de-\\r\\nfined as follows [Qin et al., 2017] [Liang et al., 2018] [Liu et\\r\\nal., 2019]:\\r\\nek\\r\\nt = Ve tanh(We[he,t−1; se,t−1] + UeXk + Be)\\r\\n(2)\\r\\nak\\r\\nt =\\r\\nexp(ek\\r\\nt )\\r\\nPN\\r\\ni exp(ei\\r\\nt)\\r\\n(3)\\r\\nwhere Ve ∈R1×l, Be ∈Rl We ∈Rl×2m Ue ∈Rl×T are\\r\\nthe parameters to be learned. l is the hidden dimension of\\r\\nthe spatial attention module. Specifically, [Qin et al., 2017]\\r\\nused l = T. m is the dimension of hidden states: he,t−1\\r\\nand se,t−1. [·; ·] is the concatenation operation. The attention\\r\\nweights Et = (ek\\r\\n1, ek\\r\\n2, ..., ek\\r\\nt )T will be treated with a softmax\\r\\nfunction to make sure the sum equals to 1.\\r\\nIn the aforementioned work, the spatial attention weights\\r\\nof the k-th sensor ek\\r\\nt are trained with the series itself across\\r\\nall time steps Xk ∈RT . The defect of this method is that the\\r\\npair-wise exogenous correlation is ignored. To overcome this\\r\\ndefect, we proposed a modified DS-RNN model, namely DS-\\r\\nRNN-II, which employed a novel spatial mechanism defined\\r\\nas follows:\\r\\nek\\r\\nt = Ve tanh(We[he,t−1; se,t−1] + UeXt + Be)\\r\\n(4)\\r\\nwhere Ve ∈R1×h, Be ∈Rh We ∈Rh×2m Ue ∈Rh×n\\r\\nare the parameters to be learned. h is the hidden dimension\\r\\nof the spatial attention module. Compared with Function 2,\\r\\nThe input series Xk is replaced with Xt, which strengthens\\r\\nthe influence of the input along spatial axes rather than tem-\\r\\nporal axes. Two spatial attention mechanism (DS-RNN and\\r\\nDS-RNN-II) will be compared in experiment in the following\\r\\nsection.\\r\\nCombining the advantages of the two aforementioned spa-\\r\\ntial attention mechanism, we further develop a hybrid spatial\\r\\nattention mechanism. The model proposed is called hybrid\\r\\ndual-stage attention based recurrent networks (hDA-RNN).\\r\\nAs shown in Function 5, the proposed hybrid attention mech-\\r\\nanism employed both Xk and Xt to train the spatial atten-\\r\\ntion weights. Considering both the k-th series over a window\\r\\nlength of T Xk and the exogenous series at current step t,\\r\\nthe attention weight is more suitable. The hybrid attention\\r\\nmechanism is defined as follows:\\r\\nek\\r\\nt = Ve tanh(We[he,t−1; se,t−1]+UeXk+U ′\\r\\neXt+Be) (5)\\r\\nak\\r\\nt =\\r\\nexp(ek\\r\\nt )\\r\\nPN\\r\\ni exp(ei\\r\\nt)\\r\\n(6)\\r\\nwhere Ve ∈R1×h, Be ∈Rh We, Le ∈Rh×2m Ue ∈Rh×T .\\r\\nhe,t−1, se,t−1 and hd,t′−1, sd,t′−1 are the hidden states of\\r\\nthe encoder and decoder respectively. In both encoder and\\r\\ndecoder layer, we use m as the dimension of hidden states for\\r\\nsimplicity.\\r\\nThe output vector of the hybrid spatial attention layer is\\r\\npresented as: (a1\\r\\ntx1\\r\\nt, a2\\r\\ntx2\\r\\nt, ..., an\\r\\nt xn\\r\\nt )T ∈Rn. The aforemen-\\r\\ntioned output vector is processed by stacked LSTM layer to\\r\\nget the encoder output: Zt = (z1\\r\\nt , z2\\r\\nt , .., zm\\r\\nt )T ∈Rm×T . The\\r\\nencoder output serves as the input of the temporal attention\\r\\nlayer.\\r\\n4.2\\r\\nTemporal attention in decoder\\r\\nThe performance of the recurrent networks is limited by its\\r\\n’memory’. The LSTM units enhance the information stor-\\r\\nage over an extended time interval by using multiplicative\\r\\ngates to regulate the access to its memory [Hochreiter and\\r\\nSchmidhuber, 1997]. However, the LSTM-based networks\\r\\nstill has difficulty in capturing long-term dependency. Hence,\\r\\nby employing a temporal attention mechanism, the capability\\r\\nof capturing long-term dependency was maximised. Because\\r\\nthe selectively weighted hidden states along the sequence are\\r\\n\\r\\nDOCUMENT: P23_Hybrid_attention_2020_preprint_OTHER_DATASET.txt\\r\\nPAGE 6\\r\\n(a) Encoder length vs MSE\\r\\n(b) Encoder length vs MAE\\r\\nFigure 3: Results of encoder window length vs both metrics in water\\r\\nflow prediction of 4 main attention-based RNN (DSTP-RNN, DS-\\r\\nRNN, DS-RNN-II and hDS-RNN).\\r\\n(a) Decoder length vs MSE\\r\\n(b) Decoder length vs MAE\\r\\nFigure 4: Results of decoder window length vs both metrics in water\\r\\nflow prediction of 4 main attention-based RNN (DSTP-RNN, DS-\\r\\nRNN, DS-RNN-II and hDS-RNN).\\r\\nmodel DS-RNN and its modified version DS-RNN-II when\\r\\nencoder length is 60.\\r\\n5.6\\r\\nEvaluation on long-term prediction\\r\\nInstead of performing the experiment with decoder length\\r\\nτ = 4, we extend the decoder length from 4 to 10 for long-\\r\\nterm prediction. The capacities of long-term prediction of\\r\\nthe four attention-based RNN model (DS-RNN, DS-RNN-II,\\r\\nDSTP-RNN and hDS-RNN) are compared. Both metrics are\\r\\nevaluated on different decoder length of the aforementioned\\r\\nmodels, as shown in Fig 4. We observe that the performances\\r\\nof the four models are almost of the same level at step 1 and\\r\\ndiffer at further steps. Remarkably, the proposed hDS-RNN\\r\\noutperforms the other baseline models at each step. Specif-\\r\\nically, the performance of DSTP-RNN trends to surpass its\\r\\ncompetitors in long-term predictions. In [Liu et al., 2019],\\r\\nexperimental results showed that DSTP-RNN performed bet-\\r\\nter in long-term prediction. However, in our case, we pay\\r\\nmore attention on short-term dependency, because in water\\r\\nflow and pressure series prediction, such long dependency is\\r\\nnot significant.\\r\\n5.7\\r\\nEvaluation on spatial attention of the model\\r\\nThe spatial distribution of geo-sensors are detailed in Figure\\r\\n5a. The spatial attention weights are presented in Figure 5b.\\r\\nIn this experiment, the target series is F8. It can be observed\\r\\nthat the input series F8 is assigned with high weights. The\\r\\nweights assigned to flow series F4 is high, the plausible rea-\\r\\nson is that their physical locations are close and they are con-\\r\\nnected directly with a long straight pipe. The weights as-\\r\\nsigned to series F5 and F6 are relatively small compared with\\r\\nthe other flow series because these two sensors are far away\\r\\nfrom the target sensor F8. The F1 F2 F3 and P1 P2 P3 are\\r\\n(a) Sensor location map.\\r\\n(b) Spatial attention weights.\\r\\nFigure 5: The map of the sensors’ location and their attention\\r\\nweights allocated in the model. In this case, ’F8’ is the target sensor.\\r\\n’F1’, ’F2’, ’P1’, ’P2’ are recorded on the outlet pipe of two remote\\r\\nbooster stations. ’F3’, ’P3’ are recorded at the nearest booster sta-\\r\\ntion. The other series are recorded on water mains\\r\\nthe flow and pressure of outflow from three booster station.\\r\\nIt can be observed that the spatial attention weights are ap-\\r\\nproximately proportional to the distance. Flow series F3 and\\r\\npressure series P3, recorded at the nearest booster station, are\\r\\nassigned with higher weights.\\r\\n6\\r\\nConclusion and future work\\r\\nIn this paper, we proposed a hybrid dual-stage attention-based\\r\\nRNN (hDA-RNN) for multivariate time series prediction in\\r\\nWDS. Following previous work, an encoder-decoder struc-\\r\\nture with dual-stage spatial-temporal attention mechanism\\r\\nwas employed. Specifically, we proposed a hybrid spatial at-\\r\\ntention mechanism in the first stage. In the proposed mech-\\r\\nanism, the spatial attention weights of a certain sensor at a\\r\\ncertain time is trained with related inputs along temporal and\\r\\nspatial axis simultaneously. Additionally, a temporal atten-\\r\\ntion mechanism is used in the second stage to adaptively al-\\r\\nlocate different weights for different time steps. Our model is\\r\\nevaluated on a dataset containing hydraulic monitoring series\\r\\nin WDS, respectively using a flow series and a pressure series\\r\\nas the target. Experimental results showed that our model\\r\\noutperformed 9 baseline models on both metrics. Spatial at-\\r\\ntention weights are visualised for a further explanation of our\\r\\napproach.\\r\\nIn the future, experiments will be conducted on various\\r\\ndatasets in different fields . In addition, a real-world appli-\\r\\ncation of our model will be conducted.\\r\\n7\\r\\nAcknowledgements\\r\\nWe would like to thank Dr. Smith for her devoted work in\\r\\npolishing the language. ```text
La separación por objetivo final cambia la lectura: ChebNet, SAM-kNN y AIGNN incluyen un predictor temporal de presión aunque su resultado final sea detectar fugas. Tsinghua describe un VAR de presión, pero no documenta con precisión el horizonte ni la disponibilidad de las entradas exógenas. Artesia estima presión nocturna a partir de agregados del día; el documento no demuestra que sean pronósticos emitidos antes de observar ese período.
```

Specifically, the L-Town water utility owns an EPANET\\r\\nmodel of the water distribution system, including nominal base demands and weekly demand patterns,\\r\\nand monitors the network through a system of 33 pressure sensors and 3 flow sensors located within\\r\\nthe system and 82 automated meters recording data at five-minute resolution located only in Area C.\\r\\nThe corresponding, pressure, flow, and demand data are available for both years 2018 and 2019.\\r\\nThe developed, pragmatic approach for leakage detection consists of the use of the aforementioned\\r\\ndata to: a) calibrate the model, in order to have a realistic representation of the hydraulic behaviour\\r\\nof the network; b) identify the total number of leakage occurred, their entity and period of occurrence\\r\\nand localize the leakage themselves by comparing the observed data against the results provided by\\r\\nthe calibrated model.\\r\\nOperatively, the model calibration phase was led by considering the 2018 dataset and followed the\\r\\nsteps listed below:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. evaluation of yearly demand pattern with seasonal variation;\\r\\nPAGE 2\\r\\n2. creation of two separate hydraulic simulation models, one for Area A+B and one for Area C;\\r\\n3. evaluation of the yearly leakage pattern for Area A+B and Area C;\\r\\n4. calibration of Area A+B and Area C hydraulic simulation models.\\r\\n\\r\\nThe subsequent leakage detection phase was led by using the calibrated models and the 2019 dataset,\\r\\nfollowing the steps listed below:\\r\\n\\r\\n1. evaluation of the yearly leakage pattern for Area A+B and Area C;\\r\\n2. identification of leakage start time and the pipe affected by the leakage.\\r\\n\\r\\nRegarding the model calibration phase, a first step included the evaluation of yearly demand patterns\\r\\nwith a seasonal variation, given that the water distribution system is located in the Northern\\r\\nhemisphere, thus water demand is expected to be higher at summer and lower at winter. Taking the output of the\\r\\nspatial attention layer Z = (z1, z2, ..., zT ) ∈Rm×T and the\\r\\nhidden states of the decoder at time t-1 hd,t−1, sd,t−1, the\\r\\ndynamic temporal attention weights are defined as follows:\\r\\nft′,t = Vd tanh(Wd[hd,t′−1; sd,t′−1] + Udzt + Bd)\\r\\n(7)\\r\\nwhere Vd ∈R1×m, Bd ∈Rm, Wd ∈Rm×2, Ud ∈Rm×m\\r\\nare the parameters to be trained.\\r\\nThe temporal attention\\r\\nweight ft′,t represents the importance of the values in encoder\\r\\nat time t for the prediction of the values in decoder at time t′.\\r\\nThe temporal attention weights are normalised by a softmax\\r\\nfunction defined as follows:\\r\\nβt′,t =\\r\\nexp(ft′,t)\\r\\nPT\\r\\nj=1 exp(ft′,j)\\r\\n(8)\\r\\nThe output of the temporal attention layer is presented as:\\r\\nζt′ =\\r\\nT\\r\\nX\\r\\nt=1\\r\\nβt′,tZt\\r\\n(9)\\r\\n4.3\\r\\nEncoder Decoder & model training\\r\\nIn this section, the encoder-decoder structure and whole train-\\r\\ning process will be summarised. In the encoder stage, Xt ∈\\r\\nRn is used as the model input. After processed by spatial\\r\\nattention layer, the middle states are Zt ∈Rm which will\\r\\nbe used as the input of the decoder. Processed by temporal\\r\\nattention layer, the final output of the model ˆyt+1 is obtained.\\r\\nAll the deep neural networks use hyperbolic tangent (tanh)\\r\\nfunction as the activation function to generate no-linear rep-\\r\\nresentation defined as follows:\\r\\ntanh(x) = exp(x) −exp(−x)\\r\\nexp(x) + exp(−x)\\r\\n(10)\\r\\nThe LSTM units are used as the basic elements in the\\r\\nmodel. LSTM aims at learning a mapping function as fol-\\r\\nlows:\\r\\nht, ct = f(Xt, ht−1, st−1)\\r\\n(11)\\r\\nwhere Xt is the input at time t, ht and ct are the hidden state\\r\\nand cell state of LSTM unit at time t-1.\\r\\nA dropout technique is used to overcome overfitting.\\r\\nAll the deep neural networks are smooth and differentiable,\\r\\nwhich makes them trainable via backpropagation algorithm\\r\\n[Rumelhart et al., 1986]. The mean squared error (MSE) be-\\r\\ntween the predicted series and the ground truth series is min-\\r\\nimized by Adam optimizer [Kingma and Ba, 2017] and the\\r\\nlearning rate is selected accordingly.\\r\\nAll deep neural networks are implemented in PyTorch\\r\\nframework.\\r\\n5\\r\\nExperiments\\r\\nIn this section, the details of the dataset used will be pre-\\r\\nsented first along with the partition of the training set, vali-\\r\\ndation set and test set. Secondly, a pretreatment process will\\r\\nbe presented to transform the unstationary series into station-\\r\\nary series. Then, the evaluation metrics and hyperparame-\\r\\nter selection will be detailed. After that, the comparison of\\r\\nthe baseline models and the proposed hDS-RNN will be pre-\\r\\nsented along with some remarks. Finally, the experiments for\\r\\na further explanation of the spatial-temporal mechanism em-\\r\\nployed are conducted and the results are illustrated.\\r\\n5.1\\r\\nDatasets\\r\\nThe experiment was conducted over a real dataset collected\\r\\nby the water supply company of the city of CZ. Sensors de-\\r\\nployed in the WDS continuously collected hydraulic param-\\r\\neters (e.g., flow rate and pressure) for systematic monitoring.\\r\\nThe dataset ranges from 05/05/2017 to 04/04/2019 with a\\r\\ntime interval of 30 minutes. The dataset contains time se-\\r\\nries of 11 flow rate monitoring sensors and 7 pressure mon-\\r\\nitoring sensors. The 11 flow sensors and 7 pressure sensors\\r\\nare placed either on the outlet pipes of booster stations or on\\r\\nwater mains. The flow sensor No.8 (F8) and pressure sensor\\r\\nNo.5 (P5) are respectively regarded as the target sensors. The\\r\\ndataset was partitioned into the training set, validation set and\\r\\ntest set. The three sets are split at 09/01/2018 and 01/01/2019,\\r\\nwith the scale of approximately 4:1:1.\\r\\n5.2\\r\\nPretreatment\\r\\nThe nonstationarity nature of the input series makes predic-\\r\\ntion difficult. Nonstationary series can be decomposed into\\r\\nTrend, Seasonal and Residual [Oliveira et al., 2017]. To\\r\\nsimplify the forecasting task, the input series is transformed\\r\\ninto its 1st order derivative to remove Trend.\\r\\nThen the\\r\\nderivatived series is additively decomposed into Seasonal\\r\\nand Residual. The decomposition process is performed only\\r\\non the training set and the seasonal pattern extracted is ap-\\r\\nplied to both validation set and test set. The residual series\\r\\nis the gap between the 1st order derivative series and the sea-\\r\\nsonal series. Our aim is to predict the residual series to recon-\\r\\nstruct the original series.\\r\\n5.3\\r\\nEvaluation metrics & Hyperparameters\\r\\nMean squared error (MSE) and mean absolute error (MAE)\\r\\nare used to evaluate the models. Their effectiveness has been\\r\\nproved by numerous time-series predicting problems [Liang\\r\\net al., 2018]. Note that yt ∈Rτ is the ground truth series and\\r\\nˆyt ∈Rτ is the predicted series. The two criteria are defined\\r\\nas:\\r\\nRMSE =\\r\\nv\\r\\nu\\r\\nu\\r\\nt 1\\r\\nN\\r\\nT +τ\\r\\nX\\r\\nt=T\\r\\n(ˆyt −yt)2\\r\\n(12)\\r\\nMAE = 1\\r\\nN\\r\\nT +τ\\r\\nX\\r\\ni=T\\r\\n(ˆyt −yt)\\r\\n(13)\\r\\nSpecifically, because the series is decomposed additively, the\\r\\nMAE and MSE score applied to either residual series or re-\\r\\nconstructed series are the same.\\r\\nDuring the training process, the learning rate for the Adam\\r\\noptimiser is 0.001. The mini-batch training is employed for\\r\\nall the deep neural networks. Although small-batch training\\r\\ncan improve the generalisation performance, it degrades com-\\r\\nputational parallelism [Masters and Luschi, 2018]. Consider-\\r\\ning both the training speed and performance, a series of batch\\r\\nPAGE 5\\r\\nsizes are tested in a preliminary experiment and a batch size\\r\\nof 64 is selected.\\r\\nThe encoder length T is grid searched over {5, 10, 20, 40,\\r\\n80}. Experiments showed that extending encoder length does\\r\\nnot improve the performance. As a result, an encoder length\\r\\nof 60 is selected. Considering the error accumulation and in-\\r\\nterval length of our dataset, the decoder length is empirically\\r\\nset to 4 for future prediction. A decoder length of 4 represents\\r\\n2 hours in the real world and is long enough for short-term\\r\\noperational decision-making.\\r\\nFinally, a grid search over {32, 64, 128, 256} is conducted\\r\\nfor hidden states dimension. Note that the hidden states in the\\r\\nencoder and decoder are the same. Meanwhile, a grid search\\r\\nover {1, 2, 4} is conducted for the number of hidden layers.\\r\\nThe hDA-RNN model which uses 60 as encoder length, 64 as\\r\\nhidden states dimension and 1 as hidden layer number, out-\\r\\nperformed the other parameter settings on the validation set.\\r\\n5.4\\r\\nModel Comparison\\r\\nIn this section, the performances of the proposed spatial-\\r\\ntemporal attention model and the other baseline models are\\r\\ncompared. The models are compared on the aforementioned\\r\\ndataset. Flow series (F8) and pressure series (P5) are used as\\r\\nthe target series.\\r\\n1. SARIMA [Arandia et al., 2016]:\\r\\nSeasonal autore-\\r\\ngressive integrated moving average model (p, d, q) ×\\r\\n(P, D, Q)s. We use s=48 because the period is 24 hours\\r\\nand the time interval is 1/2 hour. d = 1 which means 1st\\r\\norder derivative is used to erase the trend component.\\r\\nThe other parameters are optimised according to experi-\\r\\nmental results.\\r\\n2. SVR [Msiza et al., 2007]: Supported vector regressor, a\\r\\ntraditional machine learning method widely used in re-\\r\\ngression tasks.\\r\\n3. GBRT [Friedman, 2000]: An ensemble of simple mod-\\r\\nels (regression tree) proven efficient and easy to adapt in\\r\\nregression tasks.\\r\\n4. MLP [Koskela et al., 1996]: Multilayer perceptrons\\r\\n(MLP), the simplest neural networks for time series\\r\\nproblem.\\r\\n5. Seq2Seq [Sutskever et al., 2014]: The sequence to se-\\r\\nquence model is originally used in machine translation.\\r\\nNow it is slightly modified for multivariate time series\\r\\nprediction.\\r\\n6. DA-RNN [Qin et al., 2017]: DS-RNN uses dual-stage\\r\\nspatial-temporal attention-based RNN in multivariate\\r\\ntime series prediction. It was the state-of-the-art model\\r\\nbefore DSTP-RNN.\\r\\n7. DA-RNN-II: A modified dual-stage spatial-temporal\\r\\nattention-based RNN model using the spatial attention\\r\\nmechanism mentioned in Section 4.\\r\\n8. DSTP-RNN [Liu et al., 2019]: DSTP-RNN uses a dual-\\r\\nstage two-phase spatial-temporal attention mechanism\\r\\nand is the state-of-the-art model for multivariate series\\r\\nprediction.\\r\\nModels\\r\\nFLow\\r\\nPressure\\r\\nMSE\\r\\nMAE\\r\\nMSE\\r\\nMAE\\r\\nSARIMA\\r\\n3.78 E-3\\r\\n4.18 E-2\\r\\n1.41 E-2\\r\\n8.88 E-2\\r\\nSVR\\r\\n5.47 E-3\\r\\n4.89 E-2\\r\\n1.77 E-2\\r\\n9.97 E-2\\r\\nGBRT\\r\\n5.50 E-3\\r\\n4.90 E-2\\r\\n1.72 E-2\\r\\n9.80 E-2\\r\\nANN\\r\\n3.44 E-3\\r\\n4.25 E-2\\r\\n1.21 E-2\\r\\n8.32 E-2\\r\\nSeq2Seq\\r\\n3.28 E-3\\r\\n4.13 E-2\\r\\n1.27 E-2\\r\\n8.48 E-2\\r\\nTransformer\\r\\n3.09 E-3\\r\\n4.01 E-2\\r\\n1.96 E-2\\r\\n10.4 E-2\\r\\nDS-RNN\\r\\n2.17 E-3\\r\\n3.37 E-2\\r\\n1.21 E-2\\r\\n8.30 E-2\\r\\nDS-RNN-II\\r\\n2.12 E-3\\r\\n3.34 E-2\\r\\n1.23 E-2\\r\\n8.46 E-2\\r\\nDSTP-RNN\\r\\n2.70 E-3\\r\\n3.72 E-2\\r\\n1.29 E-2\\r\\n8.65 E-2\\r\\nhDS-RNN\\r\\n2.08 E-3\\r\\n3.30 E-2\\r\\n1.17 E-2\\r\\n8.18 E-2\\r\\nTable 1: Performance comparison of different models\\r\\nThe performance of all models tested is shown in Table 1.\\r\\nIn terms of flow series prediction, the proposed hybrid spatial-\\r\\ntemporal attention model (hDS-RNN) outperforms all base-\\r\\nline models. Remarkably, compared with the state-of-the-art\\r\\nDS-RNN, hDS-RNN model shows 4.1% and 2.1% improve-\\r\\nments MSE and MAE. The self-attention module addresses the limited ability of standard graph convolutions to capture long-range spatial dependencies among nodes, significantly improving spatial representational capacity. The external-attention module explicitly models the relationships between nodal attributes and pressure observations, enhancing the model's physical relevance to WDSs. 𝐷 is the dimension of the concatenation, preventing gradient explosion.\n\n## 3. Experimental setups\n\n### 3.1. Dataset\n\nReal-world WDSs typically lack comprehensive nodal monitoring, preventing direct validation of demand forecasts at non-instrumented nodes. To address this limitation and assess model performance under realistic conditions, this study adopt the benchmark l-Town Network dataset [30]. As depicted in Fig. 2(a), this network has substantial elevation variations, with a maximum difference of 74.2 m, and is serviced by two water tanks. It includes notable high and low areas where water pressure is regulated by a pressurized pumping station and a pressure-reducing valve, respectively. This network essentially covers the characteristics found in typical real WDSs.\n\nImage: Fig 2\n\n  1. Download: Download high-res image (1MB)\n  2. Download: Download full-size image\n\nFig. 2. Nodal features of l-Town Network. (a) Nodal height features (b) Nodal type features (c) Variation of different water usage types over a year.\n\nThe l-Town benchmark network comprises 50 predefined residential and commercial water demand patterns, along with 4 industrial demand patterns. To enhance the model’s relevance to real-world scenarios, this study slightly modified the l-Town network. Specifically, this study randomly assigned these 104 demand patterns to the 782 demand nodes. Fig. 2(b) depicts the water usage type at each demand node; Fig. 2(c) illustrates all water demand pattern curves, with grey lines representing individual patterns and the red line indicating their mean values. A total of one year of sequence data with a time step of 5 min was generated. The training, validation, and testing datasets were constructed by chronologically assigning 70 %, 20 %, and 10 % of the total samples, respectively.\n\nThe pressurized pumping station that provides additional pressure to the high zone is represented as a node with known pressures for prediction. Its nodal pressures were obtained from pump characteristic curves and flow sensors installed on the pump. In the weighted adjacency matrix, the two pipelines connected to the “pumping station node” are represented by a sufficiently large number (300 in this example), while the pressure-reducing valve is represented by a sufficiently small number (1 in this example).\n\n### 3.2. Model settings\n\nFor the forecasting tasks, this study adopted the GHR proposed by Zhou et al. [37] and the GGNN used by Li et al. [18] to effectively compare our proposed AGN model with previous studies. Especially, this study combined the GHR’s state estimation results with the GRU time prediction algorithm (GHR+GRU) to achieve forecasting task and extended the GGNN from water quality to the task of nodal demand.\n\nFor all three models (AGN, GGNN, and GRU), the hidden layer dimension was set to 12. All three neural networks were trained using the ADAM optimizer with a training batch size of 32. The initial learning rate was set to 1e-3, and it was reduced by a factor of 0.8 after 1 training epoch. If the training error does not decrease over 10 batches, the epoch will be terminated early. For the AGN and GGNN, the number of recurrent layers was set to 3. The forecasting step length is set to be equal to the entered historical step length.\n\n### 3.3. Evaluation metric\n\nThis study reported the Mean Absolute Error (MAE) and Mean Absolute Percentage Error (MAPE) of forecasting results for all 783 nodes (including 1 “pumping station node”) in different forecasting time steps, to evaluate the model's spatial generalization and time series forecasting effect.
